From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n17\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF40.231, F40.248\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hello, Doctor. Thank you so much for fitting me in. I do hope I'm in the right place... I feel a little foolish even mentioning it, to be honest. It's just a little quirk I have, but my family thought I should ask someone about it. \n\nYou see, whenever a... well, whenever a bridge crossing becomes unavoidable, I get a bit worked up. Well, maybe more than a bit. The moment I know we have to go over one, my heart just starts pounding away. My hands go all damp on the steering wheel—or the armrest if my daughter is driving. I just get this... this terrible rush of fear before I’m even on the bridge itself. \n\nI know it sounds silly for a man my age to be bothered by something so ordinary... I hope it's okay that I'm bringing this to you?\n\nDoctor: Is it just bridges that trigger this fear, or are there other types of crossings or situations that cause a similar reaction?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, it's really just the bridges. I hope that's helpful? I don't want to sound like a fussy old man who's afraid of everything, you know. Tunnels, planes, heights in a building... I'm perfectly fine with all of that. It's just those bridges. \n\nAnd the silly thing is, I know perfectly well that thousands of cars cross those bridges every single day without a hitch. I'm a rational man, I really am. But... well, when I see a high span on the map, or if I spot it coming up ahead through the windshield... I don't know, it just feels to me as if the whole thing is waiting to drop away right under us. \n\nIt's a dreadful feeling, even though I know better. Does that sound entirely crazy to you, Doctor? I do hope I'm explaining it properly... I just don't want you to think I've completely lost my marbles.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing this fear of bridges?\n\nPatient: Well, it hasn't been a lifelong thing, I assure you. I used to drive over them all the time without a second thought. It's just... well, it started a couple of years ago. Two years and five months, to be exact. \n\nI feel a bit foolish bringing it up, but... a dear friend of mine passed away after an accident near a bridge route. It wasn't even on the bridge itself, really, so I know it doesn't make logical sense to be so bothered by them now. I'm sorry, I hope I'm not oversharing or making too much of it. It's just that ever since then, my mind sort of... attaches that terrible event to the bridges themselves. \n\nDoes that make sense, Doctor? I do hope you don't think I'm just being overly dramatic about the whole thing.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself going out of your way to avoid bridges when you can?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, I suppose I do. I hope that's not too unusual? I mean, for over two years now, I've just... found it easier to plan my errands and visits around routes that don't have them. It's just a small adjustment, really. \n\nThough, I suppose I have given up a few things. My trips out to the San Juan Islands, for instance, and those lovely theater nights down in Seattle. I just... I'd rather not put myself through the fuss, you know? It's silly, I know, missing out on things because of a bridge. \n\nAnd, well, if I absolutely must cross one—if there's simply no other way—I'm afraid I become a bit of a nuisance. I have to study the route beforehand, make sure the timing is just right so there's no traffic... and I really must have someone in the car with me. Even then, I just sit there, quite frightened, the whole way across. \n\nI know it sounds like a lot of trouble for nothing. I try not to let it bother anyone else too much. Do you think that's a terrible way to handle it, Doctor? I just want to do the right thing.\n\nDoctor: Has your fear of bridges affected your relationships with family or friends in any way?\n\nPatient: Well, I... I suppose it has, a little bit. I mean, I try very hard not to be a burden, I really do. But... well, as I mentioned, I've had to give up those trips to the islands, and the theater outings with friends. I used to be quite active, you know. I even had some volunteer commitments that I... well, I had to step away from them because the drive was just too much for me now. \n\nI hope my friends understand. I always tell them I'm just getting a bit older and prefer to stay closer to home. I don't want them to think I'm making a fuss over nothing. But... I suppose my days have become a bit smaller, if I'm being honest. And I do have to rely on my children or friends to drive me if we're going somewhere that involves a bridge, whereas I used to handle all those plans myself. \n\nI feel terrible asking them to chauffeur me around like that. Do you think I'm asking too much of them, Doctor? I just want to make sure I'm not being completely unreasonable.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had to cross a bridge despite your fear, and if so, how did you manage to do it?\n\nPatient: Well, I... I try my best to avoid it, as I said. But there was one time, about a year ago, when my grandson was graduating. It was over in Bellevue, and there was simply no way around it without taking a very long detour that would have made us late. \n\nI didn't want to ruin his special day, you know? I wanted to be a good grandfather and just... push through it. So, my daughter drove. I made sure I sat in the back seat, right in the middle, and I just... I closed my eyes the entire time. I held onto the seatbelt so tightly my hands ached afterward. I tried humming a little tune to myself, just very quietly, so I wouldn't disturb my daughter's driving. \n\nI was trembling quite a bit, and I think I might have been crying just a little, though I tried very hard to hide it. I just kept telling myself it would be over soon. \n\nI managed it, but... I was quite exhausted for the rest of the day. I hope I didn't put a damper on the celebration. Do you think that was an alright way to handle it, Doctor? I just didn't want to make a scene and ruin things for everyone else.\n\nDoctor: Is it the sight of the bridge itself that triggers your fear, or is it more the knowledge that you will be crossing it?\n\nPatient: Well, it's... I suppose it's a bit of both, really. I hope that's not too confusing an answer? \n\nI mean, I know perfectly well that thousands of cars cross those bridges every single day without any trouble at all. I know it's perfectly safe. But... well, when I'm looking at a map and I see a high span on the route, or if we're driving and I see it looming ahead through the windshield... it just feels to me as if the whole thing is waiting to drop away right under us. \n\nIt's a dreadful feeling, really. I try so hard to just be sensible about it, to remind myself of the engineering and all that. I know it sounds terribly foolish for a man my age to be so spooked by a piece of concrete and steel. I try not to let on just how much it bothers me when I see them. Do you think it's normal to feel that way, Doctor? I just want to make sure I'm not completely losing my grip on things.\n\nDoctor: Is it the sight of the bridge that scares you, or is it the thought of having to drive over it that causes your fear?\n\nPatient: Well, I... I suppose I did just try to explain that a moment ago, didn't I? I'm so sorry if I wasn't clear enough, Doctor. I really don't mean to be difficult or make you repeat yourself. \n\nI think... well, it's really both. Just seeing it ahead of me on the road makes my stomach tie up in knots, but... just the thought of having to go over it, even before we leave the house, that's enough to start the panic, too. \n\nI do try my best to keep it in perspective, I really do. I know it's silly. Did that answer your question better this time? I just want to make sure I'm giving you the information you need.\n\nDoctor: Is there something specific about the bridge's appearance, such as its height or the water below, that contributes to your fear?\n\nPatient: Well, I... I suppose it's just the whole thing, really. I don't know if I could point to one specific part and say, \"That's the culprit.\" \n\nI mean, the height certainly isn't... pleasant, but I wouldn't say it's just the height, or just the water. It's more the... the entire structure, the whole idea of it. I try not to focus on the details too much, honestly, because I know it's all perfectly safe and sound. I don't want to make a fuss over the water or the drop when I know the engineers have sorted all that out. \n\nI'm sorry, I don't think I'm giving you a very good answer, am I? I just haven't really broken it down into pieces like that. I hope that's alright? I'm trying to be as helpful as I can, I promise.\n\nDoctor: Does the fear occur if you are crossing the bridge as a pedestrian, or is it only when you are in a vehicle?\n\nPatient: Well, I... I haven't really tried walking across one in years, to be perfectly honest. My knees aren't quite what they used to be, you see, so I don't do much walking anywhere, let alone over a bridge. \n\nBut I imagine... well, I suppose it would be just as dreadful, if not worse, being out in the open like that. I don't know for sure, though. I'm sorry I can't give you a more definite answer on that one. I hope that's not a problem? I'm just trying to be as accurate as I can for you.\n\nDoctor: Would your fear be less intense if you were on a bus or a train crossing the bridge, rather than in a car?\n\nPatient: Well, I... I haven't really been on a bus or a train in quite some time, so I'm not entirely sure. My son usually drives me wherever I need to go these days. \n\nI suppose... maybe it would be a tiny bit better if I wasn't the one in the front seat of a car, where you can see everything so clearly. But to be completely honest, I think my stomach would still do flips just knowing we were going over it. \n\nI'm so sorry, I feel like I'm giving you a lot of \"I don't knows\" today. I really hope I'm not making your job too difficult. Is that alright? I just want to make sure I'm answering you properly.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned a friend passing away near a bridge route, do you think that event triggered your fear of bridges?\n\nPatient: Well, I... I suppose you might be right about that. It's not something I like to dwell on, you know, because I don't want to seem dramatic or make a fuss over it. \n\nBut yes, I think that's when it all started. It's been... oh, about two years and five months now since he passed away after that accident. I try not to connect the two things in my head because, well, it feels silly to be so frightened of a perfectly safe road just because of what happened to him. \n\nI hope I'm not sounding too foolish, putting it all together like that. Does that make sense to you? I'm just trying to give you the whole picture, like you asked.\n\nDoctor: Would your fear of bridges be present if you were a passenger in a car being driven by someone else, but not crossing the bridge, just driving near it?\n\nPatient: Well, I... I suppose driving near it wouldn't be quite as bad. If we're not actually going over the edge of the water, I think I could manage it without making too much of a scene. \n\nMy son took me past the old highway bridge a few months ago, and... well, I did feel a little tightness in my chest, just seeing it looming up there. But I kept quiet and looked out the other window, and it wasn't unbearable. I certainly wouldn't want to ruin a perfectly good drive just because we were near one. \n\nI hope that's the kind of detail you're looking for? I just want to be sure I'm not overstating things. I know it's probably a bit silly to even feel nervous just looking at them from a distance. Does that sound like a normal reaction to you?\n\nDoctor: Is it the visual appearance of the bridge, such as its size or design, that triggers your fear?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose the look of it does have something to do with it, yes. I... I don't want to sound completely irrational, because I know perfectly well that thousands of cars cross those bridges every day without a single problem. It's a perfectly safe design, I'm sure of it.\n\nBut... well, when I see a high span on the map, or if I spot one looming up ahead through the windshield... it's just this dreadful feeling. It feels to me as if the whole thing is just... waiting to drop away right from under us. I know that's probably a terrible way to describe it, and I'm sorry if it sounds entirely foolish for a man my age to say something like that. \n\nI try to keep it to myself, of course, because I don't want to be a bother to anyone driving. I hope I'm explaining this right for you? I just want to make sure I'm giving you the right sort of answers.\n\nDoctor: Is it the sight of the bridge itself that triggers your fear, or is it something else entirely?\n\nPatient: Well, I... I suppose it's a bit of both, really. The sight of it certainly doesn't help, as I mentioned, but... I don't think it's just the bridge itself. \n\nI mean, I'm not entirely sure how to put it into words without sounding a bit dramatic. I suppose it's just... the whole situation. The thought of being up there, the water below, what happened to my friend... it all just sort of tangles together in my mind. \n\nI don't mean to be vague, I really am trying to give you a clear answer. I just haven't quite sorted out all the pieces myself, I suppose. I hope that's okay? I know it's not a very precise answer, and I'm sorry if it's not what you were looking for. Am I making any sense at all?\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of bridges more about the bridge as an object, or about the act of crossing over the water?\n\nPatient: Well, I... I suppose if I really think about it, it's not the bridge as an object. I mean, a bridge is just steel and concrete, isn't it? Perfectly sturdy. I know that. \n\nI think... I think it's the crossing. The being up so high, with all that water underneath. It just feels so... precarious. Even though I know it isn't, of course! I know the engineers who build these things are very clever people. \n\nBut yes, I'd say it's the act of going over the water that really sets me off. I hope I'm not repeating myself too much? I just want to make sure I'm giving you the distinction you're looking for. Does that sound like the sort of thing you usually hear from people like me?\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear would be less intense if you were in complete control of the vehicle while crossing the bridge?\n\nPatient: Well, I... I suppose I haven't really thought about it quite like that. I don't drive much anymore, you see, not at my age. My daughter usually takes me where I need to go, and she's a wonderful driver, very careful. \n\nBut, if I were the one behind the wheel... oh dear, I'm not entirely sure. I suppose having something to focus on, like the road and the steering wheel, might be a bit of a distraction? But then again, if I panicked while driving... well, that would be terribly dangerous for everyone, wouldn't it? I certainly wouldn't want to cause an accident. \n\nSo, I suppose I don't really know if it would be better or worse. I'm sorry, I know that's not a very helpful answer. I wish I could give you a more definite 'yes' or 'no'. Is it quite common for people to not know how they'd react in that sort of situation? I hope I'm not being too difficult by not having a proper answer for you.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."

Well, I... I suppose it's a bit of a silly thing, really. I know perfectly well that thousands of cars cross those bridges every day without a single problem. I know they're safe. But... well, when I see a high span on the map, or if we're driving and I see one looming ahead through the windshield... it just feels to me as if the whole thing is waiting to drop away right from under us. 

I know it sounds completely irrational, and I do apologize for sounding so foolish. It's just that visual... the sheer height of it, I suppose, that makes my stomach turn over. I really try not to let it bother me, I promise I do. Does that make any sense at all? I hope I'm answering your question properly.

I wish I could give you a more definite 'yes' or 'no'. Is it quite common for people to not know how they'd react in that sort of situation? I hope I'm not being too difficult by not having a proper answer for you.\n\nDoctor: Is it the act of crossing the bridge itself that scares you, or is it the visual appearance of the bridge that triggers your fear?\n\nPatient: Well, I... I suppose it's a bit of a silly thing, really. I know perfectly well that thousands of cars cross those bridges every day without a single problem. I know they're safe. But... well, when I see a high span on the map, or if we're driving and I see one looming ahead through the windshield... it just feels to me as if the whole thing is waiting to drop away right from under us. \n\nI know it sounds completely irrational, and I do apologize for sounding so foolish. It's just that visual... the sheer height of it, I suppose, that makes my stomach turn over. I really try not to let it bother me, I promise I do. Does that make any sense at all? I hope I'm answering your question properly.\n\n[Last Question]\nIs the fear you experience triggered by the bridge as an object that you see, or is it more about the situation of having to cross over the water?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 87 / not_in_workforce / bachelors\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Pleasing patient.\nA pleasing patient may\n1) minimize, downplay, or describe in more favorable terms symptoms and behaviors that they believe could create a negative impression,\n2) initially withhold or delay disclosing sensitive clinical information due to shame or fear of being judged, and\n3) seek confirmation that their descriptions or answers are appropriate and demonstrate a tendency to seek the doctor's approval.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Oh, well... I know we just talked about this a moment ago, and I really hope I'm not confusing things. I want to make sure I'm giving you the right information. 

I suppose... well, it's a bit of both, isn't it? Seeing it looming there, so high up, that certainly starts the fluttering in my chest. But then, actually having to go across it, knowing all that water is down below... that's when it really gets bad. 

I'm sorry, I know that's not a very clear answer. I'm probably making this much more complicated than it needs to be. Am I being helpful at all? I just want to make sure I'm explaining it the way you need me to.

It's just those bridges. \n\nAnd the silly thing is, I know perfectly well that thousands of cars cross those bridges every single day without a hitch. I'm a rational man, I really am. But... well, when I see a high span on the map, or if I spot it coming up ahead through the windshield... I don't know, it just feels to me as if the whole thing is waiting to drop away right under us. \n\nIt's a dreadful feeling, even though I know better. Does that sound entirely crazy to you, Doctor? I do hope I'm explaining it properly... I just don't want you to think I've completely lost my marbles.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing this fear of bridges?\n\nPatient: Well, it hasn't been a lifelong thing, I assure you. I used to drive over them all the time without a second thought. It's just... well, it started a couple of years ago. Two years and five months, to be exact. \n\nI feel a bit foolish bringing it up, but... a dear friend of mine passed away after an accident near a bridge route. It wasn't even on the bridge itself, really, so I know it doesn't make logical sense to be so bothered by them now. I'm sorry, I hope I'm not oversharing or making too much of it. It's just that ever since then, my mind sort of... attaches that terrible event to the bridges themselves. \n\nDoes that make sense, Doctor? I do hope you don't think I'm just being overly dramatic about the whole thing.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself going out of your way to avoid bridges when you can?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, I suppose I do. It's just a small adjustment, really. \n\nThough, I suppose I have given up a few things. My trips out to the San Juan Islands, for instance, and those lovely theater nights down in Seattle. I just... I'd rather not put myself through the fuss, you know? Do you think that's a terrible way to handle it, Doctor? I just want to do the right thing.\n\nDoctor: Has your fear of bridges affected your relationships with family or friends in any way?\n\nPatient: Well, I... I suppose it has, a little bit. I just want to make sure I'm not being completely unreasonable.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had to cross a bridge despite your fear, and if so, how did you manage to do it?\n\nPatient: Well, I... It was over in Bellevue, and there was simply no way around it without taking a very long detour that would have made us late. \n\nI didn't want to ruin his special day, you know? I wanted to be a good grandfather and just... push through it. So, my daughter drove. I made sure I sat in the back seat, right in the middle, and I just... I closed my eyes the entire time. I held onto the seatbelt so tightly my hands ached afterward. I tried humming a little tune to myself, just very quietly, so I wouldn't disturb my daughter's driving. \n\nI was trembling quite a bit, and I think I might have been crying just a little, though I tried very hard to hide it. I just kept telling myself it would be over soon. \n\nI managed it, but... I was quite exhausted for the rest of the day. I hope I didn't put a damper on the celebration. Do you think that was an alright way to handle it, Doctor? I just didn't want to make a scene and ruin things for everyone else.\n\nDoctor: Is it the sight of the bridge itself that triggers your fear, or is it more the knowledge that you will be crossing it?\n\nPatient: Well, it's... I suppose it's a bit of both, really. I hope that's not too confusing an answer? \n\nI mean, I know perfectly well that thousands of cars cross those bridges every single day without any trouble at all. I know it's perfectly safe. But... well, when I'm looking at a map and I see a high span on the route, or if we're driving and I see it looming ahead through the windshield... it just feels to me as if the whole thing is waiting to drop away right under us. \n\nIt's a dreadful feeling, really. I try so hard to just be sensible about it, to remind myself of the engineering and all that. I know it sounds terribly foolish for a man my age to be so spooked by a piece of concrete and steel. I try not to let on just how much it bothers me when I see them. Do you think it's normal to feel that way, Doctor? I just want to make sure I'm not completely losing my grip on things.\n\nDoctor: Is it the sight of the bridge that scares you, or is it the thought of having to drive over it that causes your fear?\n\nPatient: Well, I... I suppose I did just try to explain that a moment ago, didn't I? I'm so sorry if I wasn't clear enough, Doctor. I really don't mean to be difficult or make you repeat yourself. \n\nI think... well, it's really both. Just seeing it ahead of me on the road makes my stomach tie up in knots, but... just the thought of having to go over it, even before we leave the house, that's enough to start the panic, too. \n\nI do try my best to keep it in perspective, I really do. I know it's silly. Did that answer your question better this time? I just want to make sure I'm giving you the information you need.\n\nDoctor: Is there something specific about the bridge's appearance, such as its height or the water below, that contributes to your fear?\n\nPatient: Well, I... But I kept quiet and looked out the other window, and it wasn't unbearable. I certainly wouldn't want to ruin a perfectly good drive just because we were near one. \n\nI hope that's the kind of detail you're looking for? I just want to be sure I'm not overstating things. I know it's probably a bit silly to even feel nervous just looking at them from a distance. Does that sound like a normal reaction to you?\n\nDoctor: Is it the visual appearance of the bridge, such as its size or design, that triggers your fear?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose the look of it does have something to do with it, yes. I... I don't want to sound completely irrational, because I know perfectly well that thousands of cars cross those bridges every day without a single problem. It's a perfectly safe design, I'm sure of it.\n\nBut... well, when I see a high span on the map, or if I spot one looming up ahead through the windshield... it's just this dreadful feeling. It feels to me as if the whole thing is just... waiting to drop away right from under us. I just want to make sure I'm giving you the right sort of answers.\n\nDoctor: Is it the sight of the bridge itself that triggers your fear, or is it something else entirely?\n\nPatient: Well, I...
